Post-Hopf algebras, relative Rota-Baxter operators and solutions of the Yang-Baxter equation
Yunnan Li, Yunhe Sheng, Rong Tang

Abstract
. In this paper, first we introduce the notion of a post-Hopf algebra, which gives rise to a post-Lie algebra on the space of primitive elements and there is naturally a post-Hopf algebra structure on the universal enveloping algebra of a post-Lie algebra. A novel property is that a cocommutative post-Hopf algebra gives rise to a generalized Grossman-Larsson product, which leads to a subadjacent Hopf algebra and can be used to construct solutions of the Yang-Baxter equation. Then we introduce the notion of relative Rota-Baxter operators on Hopf algebras. A cocommutative post-Hopf algebra gives rise to a relative Rota-Baxter operator on its subadjacent Hopf algebra. Conversely, a relative Rota-Baxter operator also induces a post-Hopf algebra. Then we show that relative Rota-Baxter operators give rise to matched pairs of Hopf algebras. Consequently, post-Hopf algebras and relative Rota-Baxter operators give solutions of the Yang-Baxter equation in certain cocommutative Hopf algebras. Finally we characterize relative Rota-Baxter operators on Hopf algebras using relative Rota-Baxter operators on the Lie algebra of primitive elements, graphs and module bialgebra structures.



1.
Introduction

The Yang-Baxter equation is an important subject in mathematical physics
[
40
]
. Drinfeld
highlighted the importance of the study of set-theoretical solutions of the
Yang-Baxter equation in
[
12
]
. The pioneer works on set-theoretical solutions are those of Etingof-Schedler-Soloviev
[
15
]
, Lu-Yan-Zhu
[
29
]
and Gateva-Ivanova-Van den Bergh
[
19
]
. To understand the structure of set-theoretical solutions, Rump introduced braces in
[
36
]
for abelian groups, which provide involutive nondegenerate solutions. See also
[
5
,
6
]
for more details about the finite simple solutions of the
Yang-Baxter equation. Later Guarnieri and Vendramin generalized braces to the nonabelian case and introduced skew braces in
[
24
]
, which provide nondegenerate set-theoretical solutions of the Yang-Baxter equation. Recently, Gateva-Ivanova
[
18
]
used braided groups and
braces to study set-theoretical solutions of the
Yang-Baxter equation. In
[
1
]
, Angiono, Galindo and Vendramin introduced the notion of Hopf braces, generalizing Rump’s braces and Guarnieri-Vendramin’s skew-braces. Any Hopf brace produces a solution of the Yang-Baxter equation.

In this paper, we provide another approach to understand the structure of set-theoretical solutions of the Yang-Baxter equation in certain Hopf algebras. In particular, we introduce the notion of post-Hopf algebras, which naturally provide solutions of the Yang-Baxter equation in the underlying vector spaces. We also introduce the notion of relative Rota-Baxter operators on Hopf algebras, which naturally give rise to post-Hopf algebras, and thus to solutions of the Yang-Baxter equation. The whole theory is based on the fact that a cocommutative post-Hopf algebra gives rise to a generalized Grossman-Larsson product, which leads to a subadjacent Hopf algebra. Note that the classical Grossman-Larsson product was defined in the context of polynomials of ordered rooted trees
[
34
]
, and have important applications in the studies of Magnus expansions
[
8
,
13
]
and Lie-Butcher series
[
33
,
34
]
.

A post-Hopf algebra is a Hopf algebra
H
equipped with a coalgebra homomorphism from
H\otimes H
to
H
satisfying some compatibility conditions (see Definition
2.1
). Magma algebras, in particular ordered rooted trees, provide a class of examples of post-Hopf algebras. A cocommutative post-Hopf algebra gives rise to a new subadjacent Hopf algebra and a module bialgebra structure on itself. The terminology of post-Hopf algebras is justified by the fact that a post-Hopf algebra gives rise to a post-Lie algebra on the space of primitive elements. The notion of post-Lie algebras was introduced in
[
39
]
, and have important applications in geometric numerical integration
[
10
,
11
]
. In
[
14
]
, Ebrahimi-Fard, Lundervold and Munthe-Kaas studied the Lie enveloping algebra of a post-Lie algebra, which turns out to be a post-Hopf algebra. They also find that there is a new Hopf algebra structure (the subadjacent Hopf algebra) on the Lie enveloping algebra of a post-Lie algebra, by which the Magnus expansions and Lie-Butcher series can be constructed. The subadjacent Hopf algebra is also the main ingredient in our construction of solutions of the Yang-Baxter equation. Moreover, we show that cocommutative post-Hopf algebras and cocommutative Hopf braces are equivalent. As a byproduct, we obtain the notion of pre-Hopf algebras as commutative post-Hopf algebras.

Rota-Baxter operators on Lie algebras and associative algebras have important applications in various fields, such as Connes-Kreimer’s algebraic approach to renormalization
of quantum field theory
[
9
]
, the classical Yang-Baxter equation and integrable systems
[
2
,
28
,
38
]
, splitting of operads
[
3
]
, double Lie algebras
[
20
]
and etc. See the book
[
25
]
for more details. Recently, the notion of Rota-Baxter operators on groups was introduced in
[
26
]
, and further studied in
[
4
]
. One can obtain Rota-Baxter operators of weight 1 on Lie algebras from that on Lie groups by differentiation. Then in the remarkable work
[
21
]
, Goncharov succeeded in defining Rota-Baxter operators on cocommutative Hopf algebras such that many classical results still hold in the Hopf algebra level. In this paper, we introduce a more general notion of relative Rota-Baxter operators on Hopf algebras containing Goncharov’s Rota-Baxter operators as special cases. A cocommutative post-Hopf algebra naturally gives rise to a relative Rota-Baxter operator on its subadjacent Hopf algebra, and conversely, a relative Rota-Baxter operator also induces a post-Hopf algebra.

Remarkably, a relative Rota-Baxter operator on a cocommutative Hopf algebra naturally gives rise to a matched pair of Hopf algebras. In particular, for a cocommutative post-Hopf algebra, the original Hopf algebra and the subadjacent Hopf algebra form a matched pair of Hopf algebras satisfying certain good properties. Based on this fact, we construct solutions of the Yang-Baxter equation in a Hopf algebra using post-Hopf algebras as well as relative Rota-Baxter operators, and give explicit formulas of solutions for the post-Hopf algebras coming from ordered rooted trees. We further characterize relative Rota-Baxter operators using graphs in the smash product Hopf algebra and module structures.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, first we introduce the notion of post-Hopf algebras and show that a cocommutative post-Hopf algebra gives rise to a subadjacent Hopf algebra together with a module bialgebra structure on itself. Then we show that there is a one-to-one correspondence between cocommutative post-Hopf algebras and cocommutative Hopf braces. In Section
3
, we introduce the notion of relative Rota-Baxter operators and show that post-Hopf algebras are the underlying structures, and give rise to relative Rota-Baxter operators on the subadjacent Hopf algebras. In Section
4
, we show that a relative Rota-Baxter operator gives rise to a matched pair of Hopf algebras. In particular, a cocommutative post-Hopf algebra gives rise to a matched pair of Hopf algebras. Consequently, one can construct solutions of the Yang-Baxter equation using post-Hopf algebras and relative Rota-Baxter operators. In Section
5
, we give some alternative characterizations of relative Rota-Baxter operators using relative Rota-Baxter operators on the Lie algebra of primitive elements, graphs and module bialgebra structures.

Convention.

In this paper, we fix an algebraically closed ground field
{\mathbf{k}}
of characteristic 0. For any coalgebra
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon)
, we compress the Sweedler notation of the comultiplication
\Delta
as

\Delta(x)=x_{1}\otimes x_{2}

for simplicity.
Furthermore, for
n\geq 1
we write

\Delta^{(n)}(x)=(\Delta\otimes{\rm id}^{\otimes(n-1)})\cdots(\Delta\otimes{\rm id})\Delta(x)=x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{n+1}.

Let
(H,\cdot,1,\Delta,\varepsilon,S)
be a Hopf algebra.
Denote by
G(H)
the set of group-like elements in
H
, which is a group. Denote by
P_{g,h}(H)
the subspace of
(g,h)
-primitive elements in
H
for
g,h\in G(H)
. Denote by
P(H)
the subspace of primitive elements in
H
, which is a Lie algebra.
For other basic notions of Hopf algebras, we follow the textbooks
[
32
]
.

2.
Post-Hopf algebras

In this section, first we introduce the notion of a post-Hopf algebra, and show that a cocommutative post-Hopf algebra gives rise to a subadjacent Hopf algebra together with a module bialgebra structure on itself. A post-Hopf algebra induces a post-Lie algebra structure on the space of primitive elements and conversely, there is naturally a post-Hopf algebra structure on the universal enveloping algebra of a post-Lie algebra. Then we show that cocommutative post-Hopf algebras and cocommutative Hopf braces are equivalent. Finally, we introduce the notion of a pre-Hopf algebra which is a commutative post-Hopf algebra.

Recall from
[
17
,
39
]
that a
post-Lie algebra

(\mathfrak{h},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{h}},\rhd)
consists of a Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{h},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{h}})
and a binary product
\rhd:\mathfrak{h}\otimes\mathfrak{h}\to\mathfrak{h}
such that

(1)

\displaystyle x\rhd[y,z]_{\mathfrak{h}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[x\rhd y,z]_{\mathfrak{h}}+[y,x\rhd z]_{\mathfrak{h}},

(2)

\displaystyle([x,y]_{\mathfrak{h}}+x\rhd y-y\rhd x)\rhd z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x\rhd(y\rhd z)-y\rhd(x\rhd z).

Any post-Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{h},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{h}},\rhd)
has a
subadjacent Lie algebra

\mathfrak{h}_{\rhd}:=(\mathfrak{h},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{h}_{\rhd}})

defined by

[x,y]_{\mathfrak{h}_{\rhd}}\coloneqq x\rhd y-y\rhd x+[x,y]_{\mathfrak{h}},\quad\forall x,y\in\mathfrak{h},

and Eqs. (
1
)-(
2
) equivalently mean that the linear map
L_{\rhd}:\mathfrak{h}\to\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}(\mathfrak{h})
defined by
L_{\rhd,x}y=x\rhd y
is an action of the Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{h},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{h}_{\rhd}})
on
(\mathfrak{h},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{h}})
.

2.1.
Post-Hopf algebras and their basic properties

Definition 2.1
.

A
post-Hopf algebra
is a pair
(H,\rhd)
, where
H
is a Hopf algebra and
\rhd:H\otimes H\to H
is a coalgebra homomorphism satisfying the following equalities:

(3)

\displaystyle x\rhd(y\cdot z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x_{1}\rhd y)\cdot(x_{2}\rhd z),

(4)

\displaystyle x\rhd(y\rhd z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\rhd y)\big)\rhd z,

for any
x,y,z\in H
, and the left multiplication
\alpha_{\rhd}:H\to{\rm End}(H)
defined by

\alpha_{\rhd,x}y=x\rhd y,\quad\forall x,y\in H,

is convolution invertible in
{\rm Hom}(H,{\rm End}(H))
. Namely, there exists unique
\beta_{\rhd}:H\to{\rm End}(H)
such that

(5)

\alpha_{\rhd,x_{1}}\beta_{\rhd,x_{2}}=\beta_{\rhd,x_{1}}\alpha_{\rhd,x_{2}}=\varepsilon(x){\rm id}_{H},\quad\forall x\in H.

A
homomorphism
from a post-Hopf algebra
(H,\rhd)
to
(H^{\prime},\rhd^{\prime})
is a Hopf algebra homomorphism
g:H\to H^{\prime}
satisfying

g(x\rhd y)=g(x)\rhd^{\prime}g(y),\quad\forall x,y\in H.

It is obvious that post-Hopf algebras and homomorphisms between post-Hopf algebras form a category, which is denoted by
\mathsf{PH}
. We denote by
\mathsf{cocPH}
the subcategory of
\mathsf{PH}
consisting of cocommutative post-Hopf algebras and homomorphisms between them.

Remark 2.2
.

Similar axioms in the definition of a post-Hopf algebra also appeared in the definition of
D
-algebras
[
33
,
34
]
and
D
-bialgebras
[
31
]
with motivations from the studies of numerical Lie group integrators and the algebraic structure on the universal enveloping algebra of a post-Lie algebra.

Moreover, we have the following properties.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
(H,\rhd)
be a post-Hopf algebra. Then for all
x,y\in H
, we have

(6)

\displaystyle x\rhd 1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(x)1,

(7)

\displaystyle 1\rhd x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x,

(8)

\displaystyle S(x\rhd y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x\rhd S(y).

Proof.

Since
\rhd
is a coalgebra homomorphism, we have

\displaystyle x\rhd 1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x_{1}\rhd 1)\varepsilon(x_{2}\rhd 1)=(x_{1}\rhd 1)\cdot(x_{2}\rhd 1)\cdot S(x_{3}\rhd 1)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-2}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(x_{1}\rhd 1)\cdot S(x_{2}\rhd 1)=\varepsilon(x\rhd 1)1=\varepsilon(x)1.

By Eq. (
5
), we have

\alpha_{\rhd,1}\beta_{\rhd,1}=\beta_{\rhd,1}\alpha_{\rhd,1}={\rm id}_{H},

which means that
\alpha_{\rhd,1}
is a linear automorphism of
H
. On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rhd,1}^{2}x=1\rhd(1\rhd x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-4}}}{{=}}(1\rhd 1)\rhd x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-1}}}{{=}}1\rhd x=\alpha_{\rhd,1}x.

Hence,
1\rhd x=\alpha_{\rhd,1}x=x
.

Finally we have

\displaystyle S(x\rhd y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S(x_{1}\rhd y_{1})\varepsilon(x_{2})\varepsilon(y_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-1}}}{{=}}S(x_{1}\rhd y_{1})\cdot(x_{2}\rhd\varepsilon(y_{2})1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S(x_{1}\rhd y_{1})\cdot(x_{2}\rhd(y_{2}\cdot S(y_{3})))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-2}}}{{=}}S(x_{1}\rhd y_{1})\cdot(x_{2}\rhd y_{2})\cdot(x_{3}\rhd S(y_{3}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(x_{1}\rhd y_{1})(x_{2}\rhd S(y_{2}))=\varepsilon(x_{1})\varepsilon(y_{1})(x_{2}\rhd S(y_{2}))=x\rhd S(y).\qed

Now we give the main result in this section.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
(H,\rhd)
be a cocommutative post-Hopf algebra. Then

H_{\rhd}\coloneqq(H,*_{\rhd},1,\Delta,\varepsilon,S_{\rhd})

is a Hopf algebra, which is called the
subadjacent Hopf algebra
, where for all
x,y\in H
,

(9)

\displaystyle x*_{\rhd}y

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\rhd y),

(10)

\displaystyle S_{\rhd}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\beta_{\rhd,x_{1}}(S(x_{2})),

Furthermore,

(H,\cdot,1,\Delta,\varepsilon,S)
is a left
H_{\rhd}
-module bialgebra via the action
\rhd
.

Proof.

Since
\rhd
is a coalgebra homomorphism and
H
is cocommutative, we have

\displaystyle\Delta(x*_{\rhd}y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta(x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\rhd y))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta(x_{1})\cdot\Delta(x_{2}\rhd y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x_{1}\otimes x_{2})\cdot((x_{3}\rhd y_{1})\otimes(x_{4}\rhd y_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x_{1}\cdot(x_{3}\rhd y_{1}))\otimes(x_{2}\cdot(x_{4}\rhd y_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\rhd y_{1}))\otimes(x_{3}\cdot(x_{4}\rhd y_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x_{1}*_{\rhd}y_{1})\otimes(x_{2}*_{\rhd}y_{2})

for all
x,y\in H
, which implies that the comultiplication
\Delta
is an algebra homomorphism with respect to the multiplication
*_{\rhd}
. Moreover, we have

\varepsilon(x*_{\rhd}y)=\varepsilon(x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\rhd y))=\varepsilon(x_{1})\varepsilon(x_{2}\rhd y)=\varepsilon(x)\varepsilon(y),

which implies that the counit
\varepsilon
is also an algebra homomorphism with respect to the multiplication
*_{\rhd}
. Since the comultiplication
\Delta
is an algebra homomorphism with respect to the multiplication
\cdot
, for all
x,y,z\in H
, we have

\displaystyle(x*_{\rhd}y)*_{\rhd}z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x_{1}*_{\rhd}y_{1})\cdot((x_{2}*_{\rhd}y_{2})\rhd z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\rhd y_{1}))\cdot((x_{3}\cdot(x_{4}\rhd y_{2}))\rhd z)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-4}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\rhd y_{1})\cdot(x_{3}\rhd(y_{2}\rhd z))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-2}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\rhd(y_{1}\cdot(y_{2}\rhd z)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x*_{\rhd}(y*_{\rhd}z),

which implies that the multiplication
*_{\rhd}
is associative. For any
x\in H
, by (
6
) and (
7
), we have

\displaystyle x*_{\rhd}1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\rhd 1)=x_{1}\cdot\varepsilon(x_{2})=x,

\displaystyle 1*_{\rhd}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1\cdot(1\rhd x)=x.

Thus,
(H,*_{\rhd},1,\Delta,\varepsilon)
is a cocommutative bialgebra.
Since
\rhd
is a coalgebra homomorphism and
H
is cocommutative, we know that

\Delta\beta_{\rhd,x}=(\beta_{\rhd,x_{1}}\otimes\beta_{\rhd,x_{2}})\Delta,

and
S_{\rhd}
is a coalgebra homomorphism. Also, note that

\displaystyle x_{1}*_{\rhd}S_{\rhd}(x_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{post-rbb-1}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\rhd S_{\rhd}(x_{2}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{post-rbb-2}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle x_{1}\cdot(\alpha_{\rhd,x_{2}}(\beta_{\rhd,x_{3}}(S(x_{4}))))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{1}\cdot(\varepsilon(x_{2})S(x_{3}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(x)1,

and it means that

\displaystyle S_{\rhd}^{2}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(x_{1})S_{\rhd}^{2}(x_{2})=(x_{1}*_{\rhd}S_{\rhd}(x_{2}))*_{\rhd}S_{\rhd}^{2}(x_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{1}*_{\rhd}(S_{\rhd}(x_{2})*_{\rhd}S_{\rhd}(S_{\rhd}(x_{3})))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{1}*_{\rhd}\varepsilon(S_{\rhd}(x_{2}))1=x.

\displaystyle S_{\rhd}(x_{1})*_{\rhd}x_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{\rhd}(x_{1})*_{\rhd}S_{\rhd}^{2}(x_{2})=\varepsilon(S_{\rhd}(x))1=\varepsilon(x)1.

Therefore,
(H,*_{\rhd},1,\Delta,\varepsilon,S_{\rhd})
is a cocommutative Hopf algebra.

Moreover, we have

(x*_{\rhd}y)\rhd z=(x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\rhd y))\rhd z=x\rhd(y\rhd z).

Then by (
3
) and (
6
),
(H,\cdot,1)
is a left
H_{\rhd}
-module algebra. Since
\rhd
is also a coalgebra homomorphism,
(H,\cdot,1,\Delta,\varepsilon,S)
is a left
H_{\rhd}
-module bialgebra via the action
\rhd
.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

(i)

The product (
9
) generalizes the Grossman-Larsson product
[
22
,
34
,
35
]
defined in the context of (noncommutative) polynomials of (ordered) rooted trees. The Grossman-Larsson product plays important roles in the theories of Magnus expansions
[
8
]
and Lie-Butcher series
[
33
,
34
]
.

(ii)

By (
7
) and (
4
), we have

\alpha_{\rhd,x_{1}}\alpha_{\rhd,S_{\rhd}(x_{2})}=\alpha_{\rhd,x_{1}*_{\rhd}S_{\rhd}(x_{2})}=\alpha_{\rhd,\varepsilon(x)1}=\varepsilon(x){{\rm id}},\quad{\rm i.e.}\quad\beta_{\rhd,x}=\alpha_{\rhd,S_{\rhd}(x)},

as
\beta_{\rhd}
is also the convolution inverse of
\alpha_{\rhd}
.
Then we can rewrite Eq. (
10
) as

(11)

S_{\rhd}(x)=S_{\rhd}(x_{1})\rhd S(x_{2}),\quad\forall x\in H.

Example 2.6
.

Any Hopf algebra
H
has at least the following
trivial
post-Hopf algebra structure,

\displaystyle x\rhd y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(x)y,\quad\forall x,y\in H.

In the sequel, we study the relation between post-Hopf algebras and post-Lie algebras.

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
(H,\rhd)
be a post-Hopf algebra. Then its subspace
P(H)
of primitive elements is a post-Lie algebra.

Proof.

Since
\rhd
is a coalgebra homomorphism, for all
x,y\in P(H)
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta(x\rhd y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x_{1}\rhd y_{1})\otimes(x_{2}\rhd y_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1\rhd 1)\otimes(x\rhd y)+(1\rhd y)\otimes(x\rhd 1)+(x\rhd 1)\otimes(1\rhd y)+(x\rhd y)\otimes(1\rhd 1)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-1},\,\eqref{Post-3}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 1\otimes(x\rhd y)+(x\rhd y)\otimes 1.

Thus, we obtain a linear map
\rhd:P(H)\otimes P(H)\to P(H)
. By (
3
), for all
x,y\in P(H)
, we have

\displaystyle x\rhd(y\cdot z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1\rhd y)\cdot(x\rhd z)+(x\rhd y)\cdot(1\rhd z)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-3}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle y\cdot(x\rhd z)+(x\rhd y)\cdot z.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle x\rhd[y,z]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x\rhd(y\cdot z)-x\rhd(z\cdot y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y\cdot(x\rhd z)+(x\rhd y)\cdot z-z\cdot(x\rhd y)-(x\rhd z)\cdot y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[x\rhd y,z]+[y,x\rhd z].

By (
4
), we have

\displaystyle x\rhd(y\rhd z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(1\cdot(x\rhd y)\big)\rhd z+\big(x\cdot(1\rhd y)\big)\rhd z=(x\rhd y)\rhd z+\big(x\cdot y\big)\rhd z.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle[x,y]\rhd z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x\cdot y)\rhd z-(y\cdot x)\rhd z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x\rhd(y\rhd z)-(x\rhd y)\rhd z-y\rhd(x\rhd z)+(y\rhd x)\rhd z.

Therefore,
(P(H),[\cdot,\cdot],\rhd)
is a post-Lie algebra.
∎

In
[
14
,
35
]
the authors studied the universal enveloping algebra of a pre-Lie algebra and also of a post-Lie algebra. By
[
14
, Proposition 3.1, Theorem 3.4]
, the binary product
\rhd
in a post-Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{h},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{h}},\rhd)
can be extended to its universal enveloping algebra and induces a subadjacent Hopf algebra structure isomorphic to the universal enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{h}_{\rhd})
of the subadjacent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{\rhd}
.

We summarize their result in the setting of post-Hopf algebras as follows.
We do not claim any originality (see
[
14
,
35
]

for details).

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
(\mathfrak{h},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{h}},\rhd)
be a post-Lie algebra with its subadjacent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{\rhd}
. Then
(U(\mathfrak{h}),{\bar{\rhd}})
is a post-Hopf algebra, where
{\bar{\rhd}}
is the extension of
\rhd
determined by

1{\bar{\rhd}}u=u,\quad x_{1}\cdots x_{r}{\bar{\rhd}}u=x_{1}{\bar{\rhd}}(x_{2}\cdots x_{r}{\bar{\rhd}}u)-(x_{1}{\bar{\rhd}}x_{2}\cdots x_{r}){\bar{\rhd}}u

for all
x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\in\mathfrak{h}
and
u\in U(\mathfrak{h})
with
r\geq 1
.

Moreover, the subadjacent Hopf algebra
U(\mathfrak{h})_{\bar{\rhd}}
is isomorphic to the universal enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{h}_{\rhd})
of the subadjacent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{\rhd}
.

In a recent work
[
16
]
, Foissy extended any magma operation on a vector space
V
, i.e. an arbitrary bilinear map
{\,\mbox{\raisebox{0.8pt}{$\scriptstyle\circledast$}}\,}:V\otimes V\to V
,
to the coshuffle Hopf algebra
(TV,\cdot,\Delta^{\mathsf{cosh}})
as follows:

\displaystyle 1\rhd a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a,

\displaystyle x\rhd a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x{\,\mbox{\raisebox{0.8pt}{$\scriptstyle\circledast$}}\,}a,

\displaystyle(x\otimes x_{1})\rhd a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x{\,\mbox{\raisebox{0.8pt}{$\scriptstyle\circledast$}}\,}(x_{1}\rhd a)-(x{\,\mbox{\raisebox{0.8pt}{$\scriptstyle\circledast$}}\,}x_{1})\rhd a,

\displaystyle(x\otimes x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{n})\rhd a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x{\,\mbox{\raisebox{0.8pt}{$\scriptstyle\circledast$}}\,}\big((x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{n})\rhd a\big)

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{i-1}\otimes(x{\,\mbox{\raisebox{0.8pt}{$\scriptstyle\circledast$}}\,}x_{i})\otimes x_{i+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{n}\big)\rhd a,

and

\displaystyle 1\rhd 1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1,

\displaystyle x\rhd 1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle X\rhd(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(X_{1}\rhd a_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes(X_{m}\rhd a_{m}),

where
x,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},a,a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in V
and
X\in TV,~{\Delta^{\mathsf{cosh}}}^{(m-1)}X=X_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{m}
.

According to the discussion in
[
16
]
, it is straightforward to obtain the following result.

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
(V,{\,\mbox{\raisebox{0.8pt}{$\scriptstyle\circledast$}}\,})
be a magma algebra. Then
(TV,\cdot,\Delta^{\mathsf{cosh}},\rhd)
is a post-Hopf algebra.

.

Let
{\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{OT}}\}
be the free
{\mathbf{k}}
-vector space generated by
{\mathcal{OT}}
. The
left grafting operator

\curvearrowright:{\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{OT}}\}\otimes{\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{OT}}\}\to{\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{OT}}\}
is defined by

\displaystyle\tau\curvearrowright\omega=\sum_{s\in{\rm Nodes}(\omega)}\tau\circ_{s}\omega,\quad\forall\tau,\omega\in{\mathcal{OT}},

where
\tau\circ_{s}\omega
is the ordered rooted tree resulting from attaching the root of
\tau
to the node
s
of the tree
\omega
from the left. For example, we have

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.6}{\hbox to4.17pt{\vbox to12.14pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.08551pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{{}}}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.61 0 C 2.61 1.44 1.44 2.61 0 2.61 C -1.44 2.61 -2.61 1.44 -2.61 0 C -2.61 -1.44 -1.44 -2.61 0 -2.61 C 1.44 -2.61 2.61 -1.44 2.61 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

It is obvious that
({\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{OT}}\},\curvearrowright)
is a magma algebra. By Theorem
2.9
,
(T{\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{OT}}\},\cdot,\Delta^{\mathsf{cosh}},\rhd)
is a post-Hopf algebra,
where the underlying coshuffle Hopf algebra
(T{\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{OT}}\},\cdot,\Delta^{\mathsf{cosh}})
has the linear basis consisting of all ordered rooted forests and its antipode
S
is given by

S(\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\cdots\tau_{m})=(-1)^{m}\tau_{m}\tau_{m-1}\cdots\tau_{1},\quad\forall\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\dots,\tau_{m}\in{\mathcal{OT}}.

Moreover, it is the universal enveloping algebra of the free post-Lie algebra on one generator
\{\scalebox{0.6}{
\hbox to4.17pt{\vbox to4.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.08551pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.61 0 C 2.61 1.44 1.44 2.61 0 2.61 C -1.44 2.61 -2.61 1.44 -2.61 0 C -2.61 -1.44 -1.44 -2.61 0 -2.61 C 1.44 -2.61 2.61 -1.44 2.61 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.4}{0.0}{0.0}{0.4}{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}\}
. See
[
16
,
33
]
for more details about free post-Lie algebras and their universal enveloping algebras.

Let
B^{+}:T{\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{OT}}\}\to{\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{OT}}\}
be the linear map
producing an ordered tree
\tau
from any ordered rooted forest
\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{m}
by grafting the
m
trees
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{m}
on a new root

in order.

Moreover, the operation
B^{-}
extends to
T{\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{OT}}\}
by

\displaystyle B^{-}(\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{m})=B^{-}(\tau_{1})\cdots B^{-}(\tau_{m}),\quad\forall\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{m}\in{\mathcal{OT}}.

Note that the subadjacent Hopf algebra
(T{\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{OT}}\},{*_{\rhd}},\Delta^{\mathsf{cosh}},S_{\rhd})
is isomorphic to the Grossman-Larson Hopf algebra of ordered rooted trees defined in
[
22
]
. Using the left grafting operation, the multiplication
*_{\rhd}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}*_{\rhd}\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B^{-}(\mathcal{X}\rhd B^{+}(\mathcal{Y}))

for all ordered rooted forests
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
, and the antipode
S_{\rhd}
can be recursively defined by

S_{\rhd}(1)=1,\quad S_{\rhd}(\mathcal{X})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-3},\,\eqref{post-rbb-2'}}}{{=}}S(\mathcal{X})+({\rm Id}-\mu\varepsilon)(S_{\rhd}(\mathcal{X}_{1}))\rhd S(\mathcal{X}_{2}),

where
\mu
is the unit map and
\varepsilon
is the counit map.

Example 2.11
.

We classify all post-Hopf algebra structures on the smallest noncommutative and non-cocommutative Hopf algebra, namely, Sweedler’s 4-dimensional Hopf algebra

H_{4}={\mathbf{k}}\langle 1,g,x,gx\,|\,g^{2}=1,x^{2}=0,gx=-xg\rangle,

with its coalgebra structure and its antipode given by

\Delta(g)=g\otimes g,\quad\Delta(x)=x\otimes 1+g\otimes x,\quad\varepsilon(g)=1,\quad\varepsilon(x)=0,\quad S(g)=g,\quad S(x)=-gx.

Further
G(H_{4})=\{1,g\}
,
P_{1,g}(H_{4})={\mathbf{k}}x
and
P_{g,1}(H_{4})={\mathbf{k}}gx
.

Let
(H_{4},\rhd)
be a post-Hopf algebra structure on
H_{4}
. Then

\displaystyle\Delta(g\rhd g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(g\rhd g)\otimes(g\rhd g),

\displaystyle\Delta(g\rhd x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(g\rhd x)\otimes(g\rhd 1)+(g\rhd g)\otimes(g\rhd x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-1}}}{{=}}(g\rhd x)\otimes 1+(g\rhd g)\otimes(g\rhd x).

Namely,
g\rhd g\in G(H_{4})
and
g\rhd x\in P_{1,g\rhd g}(H_{4})
. Since
g\in G(H_{4})
implies that
\alpha_{\rhd,g}
is invertible by Eq. (
5
), we know that
g\rhd g=g
and
g\rhd x\in P_{1,g}(H_{4})\setminus\{0\}
. Also,

\displaystyle g\rhd(g\rhd x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-1}}}{{=}}(g(g\rhd g))\rhd x=g^{2}\rhd x=1\rhd x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-3}}}{{=}}x.

Therefore,
g\rhd x=x
or
-x
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle\Delta(x\rhd g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x\rhd g)\otimes(1\rhd g)+(g\rhd g)\otimes(x\rhd g)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-3}}}{{=}}(x\rhd g)\otimes g+g\otimes(x\rhd g).

Then
x\rhd g\in P_{g,g}(H_{4})
, and thus
x\rhd g=0
. So

\displaystyle\Delta(x\rhd x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x\rhd x)\otimes(1\rhd 1)+(g\rhd x)\otimes(x\rhd 1)+(x\rhd g)\otimes(1\rhd x)+(g\rhd g)\otimes(x\rhd x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-1},\eqref{Post-3}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(x\rhd x)\otimes 1+g\otimes(x\rhd x).

That is,
x\rhd x\in P_{1,g}(H_{4})
, and we can set
x\rhd x=ax
for some
a\in{\mathbf{k}}
.
Then

\displaystyle a(g\rhd x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x\rhd(g\rhd x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-4}}}{{=}}(x(1\rhd g)+g(x\rhd g))\rhd x=xg\rhd x=-gx\rhd x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\rhd(x\rhd x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Post-4}}}{{=}}(g(g\rhd x))\rhd x.

It implies that
g\rhd x=-x
unless
a=0
.

In summary, one can easily check that there is
the post-Hopf algebra structure
(H_{4},\rhd_{a})
for any
a\in{\mathbf{k}}
illustrated as below, such that
\alpha_{\rhd_{a}}
has the convolution inverse
\alpha_{\rhd_{-a}}
.

\rhd_{a}

1

g

x

gx

1

1

g

x

gx

g

1

g

-x

-gx

x

0

0

ax

agx

gx

0

0

ax

agx

Moreover, if
a\neq 0
, there is the post-Hopf algebra isomorphism from
(H_{4},\rhd_{a})
to
(H_{4},\rhd_{1})
mapping
g
to
g
and
x
to
ax
. Hence, the Sweedler 4-dimensional Hopf algebra has three non-isomorphic post-Hopf algebra structures

(H_{4},\varepsilon\otimes{\rm id})
,
(H_{4},\rhd_{0})
and
(H_{4},\rhd_{1})
.

2.2.
Post-Hopf algebras and Hopf braces

In this subsection, we establish the relation between Hopf braces and post-Hopf algebras.

In
[
1
]
, Angiono, Galindo and Vendramin introduced the notion of Hopf braces, generalizing Rump’s braces and their nonabelian generalizations, skew left braces
[
24
,
27
,
36
,
37
]
, which are stemmed from group theory.
All these algebraic objects have deep relations with the Yang-Baxter equation.

Definition 2.12

(
[
1
, Definition 1.1]
)
.

Let
(A,\Delta,\varepsilon)
be a coalgebra. A
Hopf brace
over
A
consist of two Hopf algebra structures
(A,\cdot,1,\Delta,\varepsilon,S)
and
(A,\circ,1,\Delta,\varepsilon,\mathcal{S})
satisfying the following compatibility condition,

(12)

a\circ(b\cdot c)=(a_{1}\circ b)\cdot S(a_{2})\cdot(a_{3}\circ c),\quad\forall a,b,c\in A.

We will simply denote a Hopf brace by
(A,\cdot,\circ)
.

Theorem 2.13
.

Let
(H,\rhd)
be a cocommutative post-Hopf algebra. Then the Hopf algebra
(H,\cdot,1,\Delta,\varepsilon,S)
and the subadjacent Hopf algebra
(H,*_{\rhd},1,\Delta,\varepsilon,S_{\rhd})
form a Hopf brace. Conversely, any cocommutative Hopf brace
(H,\cdot,\circ)
gives a post-Hopf algebra
(H,\rhd)
with
\rhd
defined by

x\rhd y=S(x_{1})\cdot(x_{2}\circ y),\quad\forall x,y\in H.

Proof.

Let
(H,\rhd)
be a cocommutative post-Hopf algebra. We only need to show that the multiplications
\cdot
and
*_{\rhd}
satisfy the compatibility condition (
12
), which follows from

\displaystyle x*_{\rhd}(y\cdot z)

\displaystyle=x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\rhd(y\cdot z))

\displaystyle=x_{1}\cdot((x_{2}\rhd y)\cdot(x_{3}\rhd z))

\displaystyle=x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\rhd y)\cdot S(x_{3})\cdot x_{4}\cdot(x_{5}\rhd z)

\displaystyle=(x_{1}*_{\rhd}y)\cdot S(x_{2})\cdot(x_{3}*_{\rhd}z),

for any
x,y,z\in H
.

Conversely, it is straightforward but tedious to check that a cocommutative Hopf brace
(H,\cdot,\circ)
induces a post-Hopf algebra
(H,\rhd)
.
∎

2.3.
Pre-Hopf algebras

A post-Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{h},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{h}},\triangleright)
reduces to a pre-Lie algebra if the Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{h}}
is abelian. More precisely, a
pre-Lie algebra

(\mathfrak{h},\rhd)
is a vector space
\mathfrak{h}
equipped with a binary product
\rhd:\mathfrak{h}\otimes\mathfrak{h}\to\mathfrak{h}
such that

(13)

\displaystyle(x\rhd y)\rhd z-x\rhd(y\rhd z)=(y\rhd x)\rhd z-y\rhd(x\rhd z),\quad\forall x,y,z\in\mathfrak{h}.

From this perspective, we introduce the notion of pre-Hopf algebras as special post-Hopf algebras.

Definition 2.14
.

A post-Hopf algebra
(H,\rhd)
is called a
pre-Hopf algebra
if
H
is a commutative Hopf algebra.

The above properties for post-Hopf algebras are still valid for pre-Hopf algebras.

Corollary 2.15
.

Let
(H,\rhd)
be a cocommutative pre-Hopf algebra. Then

H_{\rhd}\coloneqq(H,*_{\rhd},1,\Delta,\varepsilon,S_{\rhd})

is a Hopf algebra, which is called the
subadjacent Hopf algebra
, where the multiplication
*_{\rhd}
and the antipode
S_{\rhd}
are given by (
9
) and (
10
) respectively.

Moreover,
H
is a left
H_{\rhd}
-module bialgebra via the action
\rhd
.

Corollary 2.16
.

Let
(H,\rhd)
be a pre-Hopf algebra. Then its subspace
P(H)
of primitive elements is a pre-Lie algebra.

Recall that a pre-Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{h},\rhd)
also gives rise to a subadjacent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{\rhd}
in which the Lie bracket is defined by

[x,y]_{\mathfrak{h}_{\rhd}}=x\rhd y-y\rhd x,\quad\forall x,y\in\mathfrak{h}.

Corollary 2.17
.

Let
(\mathfrak{h},\rhd)
be a pre-Lie algebra with its subadjacent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{\rhd}
. Then the product
\rhd
can be extended to the one
{\bar{\rhd}}
on the symmetric algebra
{\rm Sym}(\mathfrak{h})
, making it a pre-Hopf algebra.
Moreover, the subadjacent Hopf algebra
{\rm Sym}(\mathfrak{h})_{\bar{\rhd}}
is isomorphic to the universal enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{h}_{\rhd})
of the subadjacent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{\rhd}
.

.

{\mathcal{T}}

Let
{\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{T}}\}
be the free
{\mathbf{k}}
-vector space generated by
{\mathcal{T}}
. The grafting operator
\curvearrowright:{\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{T}}\}\otimes{\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{T}}\}\to{\mathbf{k}}\{{\mathcal{T}}\}
is defined by

\displaystyle\tau\curvearrowright\omega=\sum_{s\in{\rm Nodes}(\omega)}\tau\circ_{s}\omega,\quad\forall\tau,\omega\in{\mathcal{T}},

where
\tau\circ_{s}\omega
is the rooted tree resulting from attaching the root of
\tau
to the node
s
of the tree
\omega
. For example, we have

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.6}{\hbox to4.17pt{\vbox to12.14pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-2.08551pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{{}}}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.61 0 C 2.61 1.44 1.44 2.61 0 2.61 C -1.44 2.61 -2.61 1.44 -2.61 0 C -2.61 -1.44 -1.44 -2.61 0 -2.61 C 1.44 -2.61 2.61 -1.44 2.61 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
